Homotopy and Arithmetic From bi-operads to generalized schemes
Shai Haran

Abstract
We define the concept of a Bi-operad. We develop the homotopy theory of Bital-Sets and of ∞ \infty -Bi-operad. We develop a geometry of generalized schemes based on the spectra of distributive monochromatic bi-operads.



1
Introduction

It was Dan Kan who developed the language of
Categories
, and of
Simplicial Sets
based on finite-linearly-ordered sets, to give a
combinational approach to homotopy. Dan Quillen axiomatized this approach in
his language of
Model Categories
.
Operads
were discovered by
Boardman and Vogt in connection with homotopy invariant algebraic structures, and by May
in connection with iterated loop spaces. Ieke Moerdijk (with collaborators I. Weiss, C.
Berger, D.C. Cisinski, G. Heuts, V. Hinich, E. Hoffbeck) generalized categories and operads
to a theory of “
Coloured-Operads
”, leading to a generalization of simplicial
sets to “
Dendroided Sets
”, based on finite trees, and developed the homotopy
theory of
\infty
Operads
generalizing
\infty
Categories
.

While categories and simplicial sets have a dual opposite, there is no opposite of an operad.
Some algebraic structures (such as Hopf-algebras and bi-algebras) are not controlled by an
operad since they involve both operations and “co-operations”. The purpose of this paper
is to define a notion of “Bi-Operad”, having both operations and co-operations, and of
“Bital-Sets”, based on finite - “Bi-trees”, and sketch the homotopy theory of

\infty
Bi-Operads generalizing the Dendroidal and the Simplicial stories.

Having an opposite for
Bi-operads and Bital-Sets we can speak of the self-dual Bi-operads and Bital-sets having an
involution. Finally, returning to the classical “monochromatic” case of having
one colour or object, we can speak of the distributive Bi-operads.

It was Alexander Grothendieck who develop the modern language of algebraic geometry, the
schemes
,
based on the
spectra
of commutative-rings. But commutative rings are Not adequate
for
Arithmetical Geometry
(The “integers” at a real or complex place of a
number field do not form a sub-ring; the integers
\mathbb{Z}
are the initial object of rings
and so
\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})
is the final object of Grothendieck’s geometry, rather than the

absolute point

\text{spec}(\mathbb{F}_{1})=\left\{(0)\right\}
, where

\mathbb{F}_{1}
the initial object of distributive bi-operads is the
“Field with one
element”
; the
Arithmetical surface

\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})\prod\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})=\text{spec}\left(\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\right)=\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})
reduces to its diagonal

[
32
]
) The self-dual distributive bi-operads are essentially the
“Generalized-Rings”
of

[
37
]
, and are the building blocks of an
Arithmetical Geometry
, generalizing
Grothendieck’s theory of
Schemes
.

As it turns out, to do geometry following
Grothendieck’s paradigm, one does not need an involution (although in arithmetic, the real
and complex “integers” prefer the self-dual
\ell_{2}
-metric). We lay the foundation of

generalized schemes
based on the spectra of distributive bi-operads. This new
language of geometry is indeed much simpler than the one of
[
37
]
(where we had to keep the
involution), and it also shows the way to a
“Quantum-geometry“
(where vectors
and co-vectors are Not the same).

Thus the theory of Bi-operads and Bital-sets give the homotopy
language needed for
Derived Arithmetical Geometry
and for

Arithmetical Stacks
. The
Arithmetical Surface

\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})\prod\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z})=\text{spec}(\mathbb{Z}\coprod\mathbb{Z})
with
\mathbb{Z}\coprod\mathbb{Z}
the sum in the category of
distributive Bi-operads (and its compactification as a pro-object of generalized schemes,
as in
[
34
]
) is our Jungentraum of
[
32
]

“Mathematics should not be complicated! You just set the stage right -
then you sail away…”

Raoul Bott

“What’s important is the point-of-view… the theorems will follow!”

Barry Mazur

lecture on the rule of the point at infinity in

“Algebra and Geometry”, Cambridge
1993.

The content of this report is as follows:

1.

Introduction

2.

Bio
\equiv
Bi-operands
We define Bi-operads as a pair of
colored symmetric operads with the same objects, and with mutual actions
on each other.

3.

Bit
\equiv
Bi-trees
We define the Bi-trees as finite
oriented graphs, with no closed paths, and such that for any vertex

v
,
\#d_{-}(v)=1
or
\#d_{+}(v)=1
.

4.

Formulas and
1
-sub-bits
We define our “formulas”
inductively as bi-trees with compatible planar structures.

5.

The free Bio
We describe the free bio on a collection

\mathcal{C}
as the
\mathcal{C}
-labeled formulas.

6.

Bios via generators and relations
We show Bio is complete
and co-complete.

7.

Bipo
\equiv
Bi-Pre-Order
We describe the (co-)complete
closed symmetric monoidal structure on Bipo
\equiv
the full subcategory
of Bio with objects the bios having at most one point in every hom-set.

8.

Natural transformation
We describe the internal hom
structure on Bio.

9.

Bilinear maps and tensor products
We describe the closed
monoidal structure on Bio.

10.

From Bits to Bios
We associate with every bit a bio and we
make Bit into a category by defining the “flat” maps of bits.

11.

Degeneracies and face maps
We show these maps generate
Bit; the face maps are either “outer” (
=
elimination of an edge), or
“inner” (= the contraction of “composition” or “action”
edge(s)).

12.

The category of Bital sets bSet
We describe the category

\text{bSet}\equiv\text{Set}^{\text{Bit}^{\text{op}}}
of pre-sheaves on
Bit, and the resulting Kan adjunction

bSet
Bio
.

13.

The symmetric “monoidal” structure on bSet
We describe
the closed, symmetric, and not quite associative, tensor structure on
bSet.

14.

The image of
N:\text{Bio}\hookrightarrow bSet
The nerve
functor
N
gives a full and faithfull embedding of Bio onto the
strict-Kan Bital Sets.

15.

The homotopy functor
\tau=\text{ho}:\infty\text{Bio}\to\text{Bio}
For an
\infty\text{Bio}

X\in\text{bSet}
satisfying the
(inner) Kan condition, the associated bio
\tau(X)
has a homotopical
description.

16.

Normal monomorphisms
We describe following
[
12
]
,

[
66
]
the cofibrations of bSet, and their interaction with the monoidal
structure.

17.

Quillen Model structure. Ein Märchen
Following

[
66
]
we describe the model structures on
\text{sBio}_{C}
,
C
a fixed
set of objects, and the conjectural model structures on simplicial bios

sBio
, and on bital sets
bSet
(these should be equivalent
via a modification of the Berger-Moerdijk-Boardman-Vogt resolution).

18.

Self-duality
We show the bios
\mathscr{P}
, with an involution

\mathscr{P}\cong\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}}
,
X\mapsto X^{t}
, can be also described as
operads with a “contraction” operaton, making contact with the language
of
[
37
]
.

19.

Rigs
For a rig
A
(
=
associative ring without
“negatives”, i.e. no inverses for addition) we associate a bio

\mathscr{P}_{A}
with one object, giving a full and faithful embedding

\text{Rig}\hookrightarrow\text{Bio}_{\left\{\ast\right\}}
.

20.

The
\ell_{p}
bio
For
p\in[1,\infty]
we define the
“
\ell_{p}
-sub-bio” of the bios
\mathscr{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathscr{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
; for

p=2
these are
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the real and complex
“integers”.

21.

Distributive bios
These are the monochromatic bios

\mathscr{P}
where the actions and compositions interchange; the monoid

\mathscr{P}(1)
is commutative and central. The association
A\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{A}
gives a full and faithful embedding of commutative rings into
distributive bios under
\mathbb{F}_{1}

C\text{Ring}\hookrightarrow\text{CBio}.

22.

The spectrum
We associate with a distributive bio

\mathscr{P}\in\text{CBio}
, a compact
\text{sober}\equiv\text{Zariski}
,
topological space
\text{spec}(\mathscr{P})
.

23.

The structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{P}}
We describe the
sheaf of bios over
\text{spec}(\mathscr{P})
,
\mathscr{P}\in\text{CBio}
.

24.

Generalized Schemes
Following Grothendieck
[
29
]
we
describe the generalized schemes as spaces with a sheaf of distributive
bios with local stalks, that are locally affine.

25.

A
-sets
For
A\in\text{CBio}
we define the
category of
A\text{-sets}
. For
A\in C\text{Ring}
this is the category
of
A
modules.

26.

Commutativity
We discuss commutativity of

A\text{-sets}

27.

Symmetric monoidal structure
We describe the closed
monoidal (model) structure on
CA\text{(s)Set}
-the category of
commutative
A
-(simplicial) sets.

28.

Symmetric sequences
We describe the Category

\Sigma(A)\equiv\left(CA\text{-sSet}\right)^{\text{iso}(\text{Fin})}
of
symmetric sequences, and the operadic Fourier transform.

29.

The sphere spectrum
Following
[
44
]
,

[
45
]
, we describe
the sphere spectrum
S_{F}^{.}
as the commutative-monoid-object of

\Sigma(\mathbb{F})
, giving rise to the category

S_{A}^{.}\text{-mod}\subseteq\Sigma(A)
of “symmetric spectra“, with
its stable monoidal model structure.

30.

Quasi-coherent
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-modules
We describe the
category of
\mathscr{O}_{X}\text{-modules}
,
X
a generalized scheme,
and its full subcategory
q.c.\mathscr{O}_{X}\text{-modules}
of
”quasi-coherent“
\mathscr{O}_{X}\text{-modules}
.

31.

The global derived category
Following
[
60
]
, we can
describe the derived category of quasi-coherent simplicial

\mathscr{O}_{X}\text{-modules}
as the homotopy category of the

\infty\text{-category}
of cartesian sections of the inner fibration

\text{Aff}/_{X}\ltimes S_{A}^{.}\text{-mod}\to\text{Aff}/_{X}
.

“At all events, I have exceeded my commission and been seduced into describing
things as they may be and, as seems to me at present, are likely to be… The
views of this report are in any case not peculiar mine. I have simply fused my own
observations and reflections with ideas of others and with commonly accepted
tenets.”

R.P. Langlands

2
Bio
\equiv
Bi-operads

Definition 2.1
.

A Bio (
\equiv
Bi-Operad)
\mathscr{P}=(\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{o},\mathscr{P}^{+})
is a pair

\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{+}
of (coloured, symmetric) operads with the same set of
“colours” or “objects”
\mathscr{P}^{o}
, together with mutual actions of

\mathscr{P}^{-}
,
\mathscr{P}^{+}
on each other.

Explicitly, for
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{o}
we have sets (simplicial sets, etc.) of
(co)-operations
\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
and
\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1},\dots,c_{n};c_{0})
, with
actions of the symmetric group
S_{n}
, given for
\sigma\in S_{n}

We have units
{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{-}\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(c;c)
and
{\mathbf{1}}^{+}_{c}\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(c;c)
,

(n=1)
.

The compositions are given for each word

\overline{c}=c_{11}\dots c_{1{m_{1}}},c_{21}\dots c_{2{m_{2}}},\dots,c_{n1}\dots c_{n{m_{n}}}\in\left(\mathscr{P}^{\circ}\right)^{\sum m_{i}}

partitioned into the
n
words

\overline{c_{1}}=c_{11}\dots c_{1{m_{1}}}\;,\;\overline{c_{2}}=c_{21}\dots c_{2{m_{2}}}\;,\dots\;,\;\overline{c_{n}}=c_{n1}\dots c_{n{m_{n}}}

and for
b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{o}
,

(2.3)

\begin{array}[H]{l}o:\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\dots b_{n})\times\left[\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{1};\overline{c_{1}})\times\dots\times\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{n},\overline{c}_{n})\right]\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};\overline{c})\\
\\
o:\left[\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c_{1}};b_{1})\times\dots\times\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c}_{n};b_{n})\right]\times\mathscr{P}^{+}(b_{1}\dots b_{n};b_{0})\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c};b_{0})\end{array}

These compositions are assumed to be
associative
,

(2.4)

\begin{array}[H]{rll}P\circ\big(P_{i}\circ(P_{ij})\big)&=\big(P\circ(P_{i})\big)\circ(P_{ij})&P,P_{i},P_{ij}\in\mathscr{P}^{-}\\
\\
(P_{ij})\circ\big((P_{j})\circ P\big)&=\big((P_{ij})\circ P_{j}\big)\circ P&P_{ij},P_{j},P\in\mathscr{P}^{+}\end{array}

unital
,

(2.5)

\begin{array}[H]{ll}{\mathbf{1}}_{c_{0}}^{-}\circ P=P=P\circ({\mathbf{1}}_{c_{i}}^{-})&P\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n})\\
\\
\left({\mathbf{1}}_{c_{i}}^{+}\right)\circ P=P=P\circ{\mathbf{1}}_{c_{0}}^{+}&P\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0})\end{array}

and
S_{n}\times\left[S_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times S_{m_{n}}\right]\subseteq S_{m_{1}+\dots+m_{n}}

covariant
. The mutual actions are given for such words

\overline{c}=\overline{c}_{1}\dots\overline{c}_{n}
,

\overline{c}_{i}=\left(c_{i1}\dots c_{i,{m_{i}}}\right),b_{0},b_{1}\dots b_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{o}
, by maps

(2.6)

\begin{array}[H]{lll}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,:\left[\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{1};\overline{c}_{1})\times\dots\times\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{n};\overline{c}_{n})\right]\times\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c};b_{0})\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\mathscr{P}^{+}(b_{1}\dots b_{n};b_{0})\\
\\
\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,:\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};\overline{c})\times\left[\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c}_{1};b_{1})\times\dots\times\mathscr{P}^{+}(\overline{c}_{n};b_{n})\right]\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\dots b_{n})\end{array}

These actions are assumed to be
associative

(2.7)

\big(P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{ij})\big)\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{j})=P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\big((P_{ij})\circ P_{j}\big)\quad,\quad(P_{i})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\big((P_{ij})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P\big)=\big(P_{i}\circ(P_{ij})\big)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P

unital
,

(2.8)

P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,({\mathbf{1}}_{c_{i}}^{+})=P\quad,\quad\left({\mathbf{1}}_{c_{i}}^{-}\right)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P=P

S_{n}
-
covariant
, and
S_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times S_{m_{n}}
-
invariant
.

We will also assume that these actions are “linear” in all possible ways:

(2.9)

\big(P\circ(P_{i})\big)\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{ij})=P\circ\big(P_{i}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{ij})\big)\quad,\quad(P_{ij})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\big((P_{j})\circ P\big)=\big((P_{ij})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P_{j}\big)\circ P

and

(2.10)

P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\big((P_{ij})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P_{j}\big)=\big(P\circ(P_{ij})\big)\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{j})\quad,\quad\big(P_{i}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{ij})\big)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P=(P_{i})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\big((P_{ij})\circ P\big)

A map of bios
\varphi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a pair of functors of operads

\varphi^{-}:\mathscr{P}^{-}\to\mathscr{Q}^{-}
and
\varphi^{+}:\mathscr{P}^{+}\to\mathscr{Q}^{+}
, with the same map
on objects
\varphi^{\circ}:\mathscr{P}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}
and preserving the natural actions

(2.11)

\varphi^{-}\big(P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{i})\big)=\varphi^{-}(P)\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\big(\varphi^{+}(P_{i})\big)\qquad\varphi^{+}\big((P_{i})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P\big)=\big(\varphi^{-}(P_{i})\big)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\varphi^{+}(P)

(as well as preserving the compositions, units, and
S_{n}
-actions).

Thus we have a
category
Bio
.

Remark 2.12
.

Forgetting the
S_{n}
-actions we get the notion of
planar
, or
non-symmetric bio, and we have the category
p
Bio. There is a forgetful functor
U:\text{Bio}\to p\text{Bio}
, forgetting the
S_{n}
-actions, and this functor has a left adjoint

\Sigma:p\text{Bio}\to\text{Bio}
, with free
S_{n}
-actions

\textstyle\sum\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n}):=\coprod\limits_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{\sigma(1)}\dots c_{\sigma(n)})

We say the bio
\mathscr{P}
is
closed
if (
n=0
),

\mathscr{P}^{-}(b;\phi)=\left\{0_{b}^{-}\right\}
,
\mathscr{P}^{+}(\phi;b)=\left\{0_{b}^{+}\right\}
, reduce to a point for each
b\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
; we say
\mathscr{P}
is

open
if these sets are empty. We will be mainly interested in closed bios

\mathscr{P}
; the advantage being that every
n
(co)-operation give rise to
m
(co)-operations
for
m<n
: and for
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\subseteq\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
we get maps
obtained by substituting
0_{b_{i}}^{-}
, resp.
0_{b_{i}}^{+}
, for
i\not\in I
,

\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\dots b_{n})\to\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};b_{i_{1}}\dots b_{i_{m}})\quad,\quad\mathscr{P}^{+}(b_{1}\dots b_{n};b_{0})\to\mathscr{P}^{+}(b_{i_{1}}\dots b_{i_{m}};b_{0})

We will write Bio for the category of
closed
bios, and
\text{Bio}^{\phi}
for
the category of
open
bios.
We can also fix the set of objects
C
, and we have the categories
\text{Bio}_{C}

(resp.
p\text{Bio}_{C}
) with objects the closed bios
\mathscr{P}
with
\mathscr{P}^{\circ}=C
, and maps of
bios
\varphi
such that
\varphi^{\circ}\equiv\text{id}_{C}
.

Remark 2.13
.

For a bio
\mathscr{P}
we have the underlying categories
i^{*}\mathscr{P}^{-}
and

i^{*}\mathscr{P}^{+}
, and for
f^{\pm}\in\mathscr{P}^{\pm}(b;c)
we have

(2.14)

\overrightarrow{f^{-}}:=f^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}^{+}_{c}\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(b;c)\quad\text{and}\quad\overleftarrow{f^{+}}:={\mathbf{1}}^{-}_{b}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,f^{+}\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(b;c).

These are inverse isomorphisms

(2.15)

\overset{\leftrightarrows}{f^{-}}={\mathbf{1}}_{b}^{-}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\left(f^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{+}\right)=({\mathbf{1}}_{b}^{-}\circ f^{-})\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{+}=f^{-}

and similarly
\overset{\rightleftarrows}{f^{+}}=f^{+}
. For
g^{\pm}\in\mathscr{P}^{\pm}(c;d)
we have

(2.16)

\begin{array}[H]{lll}\overrightarrow{f^{-}\circ g^{-}}&=(f^{-}\circ g^{-})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{d}^{+}=f^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,(g^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{d}^{+})\\
\\
&=f^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\big({\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{+}\circ(g^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{d}^{+})\big)=(f^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{+})\circ(g^{-}\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{d}^{+})\\
\\
&=\overrightarrow{f^{-}}\circ\overrightarrow{g^{-}}\end{array}

and similarly
\overleftarrow{f^{+}\circ g^{+}}=\overleftarrow{f^{+}}\circ\overleftarrow{g^{+}}
.

Using these canonical isomorphisms we identify
i^{*}\mathscr{P}^{-}
and
i^{*}\mathscr{P}^{+}
, and we
write
{\mathbf{1}}_{c}
for either
{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{-}
or
{\mathbf{1}}_{c}^{+}
,
c\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
. Note that for
P\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(b;c_{1}\cdots c_{n})
,

Q\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c)
, this identifies
P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,Q
with

P\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,Q
.

Remark 2.17
.

Every bio
\mathscr{P}=(\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\mathscr{P}^{+})
has an
opposite
bio

\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}}=(\mathscr{P}^{+},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\mathscr{P}^{-})
, with the same objects, and
for
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
,

(2.18)

\begin{array}[H]{l}(\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}})^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n}):=\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0}),\\
\\
(\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}})^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0}):=\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n})\end{array}

and with the opposite compositions and actions, so

(2.19)

P^{\text{op}}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\left(P_{i}^{\text{op}}\right):=\big(\left(P_{i}\right)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P\big)^{\text{op}}\quad,\quad(P_{i}^{\text{op}})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,P^{\text{op}}:=\big(P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(P_{i})\big)^{\text{op}}

This is an involution,
(\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}})^{\text{op}}=\mathscr{P}
, of Bio extending the
one on

\text{Cat}\;\;(=\text{the category of small categories})

Remark 2.20
.

For a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{E}
, we can similarly define the
category
\text{Bio}(\mathcal{E})
of
\mathcal{E}
-enriched bios

\mathscr{P}=(\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\mathscr{P}^{+})
. Here for
c_{0},c_{1}\dots c_{n}
we have
objects of
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n})
,
\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0})\in\mathcal{E}^{\circ}
, and the compositions, actions, and units, are
given by maps in
\mathcal{E}
, replacing the Cartesian product
\times
in Set by
the monoidal product
\otimes
in
\mathcal{E}
.

For an
\mathcal{E}
-enriched
operad
\mathscr{P}\in\text{Oper}(\mathcal{E})
, we get the bio
j_{!}\mathscr{P}\in\text{Bio}(\mathcal{E})
, with
j_{!}\mathscr{P}:=(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},i^{*}\mathscr{P})
, i.e. with the
same operations, but with only unaray cooperations. This is left adjoint to the
forgetfull functor
j^{*}:\text{Bio}(\mathcal{E})\to\text{Oper}(\mathcal{E})
,

j^{*}\mathscr{P}=(\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{\circ})
. There is a right adjoints
i_{*}

given by
defining the missing
m
-array operations,
m>1
to be a point. We thus have the
adjunctions

(2.21)

\text{Cat}(\mathcal{E})
i_{!}
i^{*}
i_{*}
\text{Oper}(\mathcal{E})
j_{!}
j^{*}
\text{Bio}(\mathcal{E})

and
i_{!},j_{!}
are full and faithful embeddings.

For a symmetric monoidal category

\mathcal{E}
, we have the bio
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
, with the same objects

\mathcal{E}^{\circ}
as the category
\mathcal{E}
, and with (co)-operations for

c_{0},c_{1}\dots c_{n}\in\mathcal{E}^{\circ}

\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n}):=\mathcal{E}(c_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes c_{n},c_{0})\quad,\quad\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0}):=\mathcal{E}(c_{0},c_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes c_{n})

with the obvious compositions and mutual actions (again given by compositions); this gives
an embedding

and adjunction
\mathcal{E}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2},c)=\mathcal{E}(b_{1},c^{b_{2}})
, then we can view

\hat{\mathcal{E}}
as an
\mathcal{E}
-enriched bio

(2.24)

\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n}):=c_{0}^{c_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes c_{n}}\quad,\quad\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0}):=(c_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes c_{n})^{c_{0}}

For the category of simplicial sets

\mathcal{E}=\text{sSet}=(\text{Set})^{\Delta\mkern-12.0mu\Delta^{\text{op}}}
, we shall write
\text{sBio}\equiv\text{Bio}(\text{sSet})
for simplicial bios. For the category of
(compactly generated)
topological spaces Top we shall write
\text{tBio}\equiv\text{Bio}(\text{Top})
for
topological bios.

Definition 2.25
.

A collection

\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}^{-},\mathcal{C}^{\circ},\mathcal{C}^{+})
is a
set
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
, and for
c_{0},c_{1}\dots c_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
we
have sets
\mathcal{C}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n})
and
\mathcal{C}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0})
and when
n=1
we have
\mathcal{C}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1})\equiv\mathcal{C}^{+}(c_{0};c_{1})
, and for
c_{0}=c_{1}=c
we have
{\mathbf{1}}_{c}\in\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(c;c)
.

A map of collections
\varphi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
is a function

\varphi^{\circ}:\mathcal{C}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
, and for
c_{0},c_{1}\dots c_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
functions
\varphi^{-}:\mathcal{C}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\dots c_{n})\to\mathcal{B}^{-}\left(\varphi^{\circ}(c_{0});\varphi^{\circ}(c_{1})\dots\varphi^{\circ}(c_{n})\right)
and
\varphi^{+}:\mathcal{C}^{+}(c_{1}\dots c_{n};c_{0})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}\left(\varphi^{\circ}(c_{1})\dots\varphi^{\circ}(c_{n});\varphi^{0}(c_{0})\right)
with

\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}
when
n=1
, and

\varphi^{\pm}({\mathbf{1}}_{c})={\mathbf{1}}_{\varphi^{\circ}(c)}
. Thus we have a category

Coll
, and a forgetful functor

(2.26)

U:\text{pBio}\to\text{Coll}

We shall describe next its left adjoint

(2.27)

p\mathcal{F}:\text{Coll}\to\text{pBio}

which gives the left adjoint of
U:\text{Bio}\to\text{pBio}\to\text{Coll}
, the

free bio
on
\mathcal{C}

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}=\sum p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

3
Bit
\equiv
Bi-Trees

Definition 3.1
.

A
graph

(G^{{\mathbf{1}}}
G^{\circ})
\scriptstyle d_{-}
\scriptstyle d_{+}
for us will always be finite and oriented, so is just given by two parallel functions of
finite sets:
G^{1}
the “
edges
” to
G^{\circ}
the
“
vertices
”.

As a general rule we write our edges from right

\equiv
positive to the left
\equiv
negative

(3.2)

e
d_{-}e
d_{+}e

A map of graphs
\varphi:G\to H
is a pair of functions
\varphi^{1}:G^{1}\to H^{1}
and
\varphi^{\circ}:G^{\circ}\to H^{\circ}
such that

d_{\pm}^{H}\circ\varphi^{1}=\varphi^{\circ}\circ d_{\pm}^{G}
.

Thus we have a category of (finite, oriented)
Graph
.

For
v\in G^{\circ}
, we write
d_{-}v=d_{+}^{-1}(v)
,
d_{+}v=d_{-}^{-1}(v)
.

For
G\in\text{Graph}
,
n,m\geq 0
, put
C_{n,m}(G):=\left\{v\in G^{\circ},\#d_{-}v=n,\#d_{+}v=m\right\}
.

The left (resp. right) ends of
G
,
C_{01}(G)
(res.
C_{10}(G)
), are called

stumps
, and they are in bijection with the edges attached to them,
which we will denote by

(3.3)

d_{-}(G):=d_{-}^{-1}C_{01}(G)\xrightarrow{\;\;\underset{\sim}{d_{-}}\;\;}C_{01}(G)\quad,\quad d_{+}G:=d_{+}^{-1}C_{10}(G)\xrightarrow{\;\;\underset{\sim}{d_{+}}\;\;}C_{10}(G)

We say
G
is a
{\mathbf{1}}
-Graph if

(3.4)

C_{nm}(G)\not=\phi\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;n=1\text{ or }m=1

Thus for a
{\mathbf{1}}
-graph
G

(3.5)

\begin{array}[H]{rll}G^{\circ}&=C_{01}(G)\coprod C^{-}(G)\coprod C^{+}(G)\coprod C_{10}(G)\\
\\
C^{-}(G)&=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}C_{1m}(G)\quad,\quad C^{+}(G)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}C_{n1}(G)\end{array}

Such a
{\mathbf{1}}
-graph is made up of the basic negative and positive

n
-
corollas
,
n\geq 0
,

(3.6)

C_{n}^{-}
:
e_{0}
e_{1}
e_{n}
C_{n}^{+}
:
e_{n}
e_{1}
e_{0}

A
path
in a graph
G
, “from
v_{m}
, resp.
e_{m}
, to
v_{0}
, resp.
e_{1}
”,

e_{i}\in G^{1}
,
v_{i}\in G^{0}
, is given by

(3.7)

\leq
on
G^{1}\coprod G^{\circ}
:

(3.8)

v,\;\text{resp. }e\leq v^{\prime},\;\text{resp. }e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\exists\;\text{path from $v^{\prime}$, resp. $e^{\prime}$, to $v$, resp. e.}

Every path in a simple graph
G
extends to maximal path.

A path
\ell=(e_{1},\cdots,e_{m})

is maximal iff
e_{1}\in d_{-}G
,
e_{m}\in d_{+}G
.

Definition 3.9
.

A
Bit
(
\equiv\text{Bi-Tree}
)
G
is a simple
{\mathbf{1}}
-graph.

We write

\begin{array}[H]{ll}\text{Bit}^{k}_{n,m}&=\left\{G\in Bit,\;\#d_{-}G=n,\;\#d_{+}G=m,\;\#G^{\circ}=n+m+k\right\}/\text{isom}\\
\\
\text{Bit}_{n,m}&=\bigcup_{k}\text{Bit}_{n,m}^{k}\quad,\quad\text{Bit}=\bigcup_{n,m}\text{Bit}_{n,m}\\
\\
\text{Bit}^{-}&=\bigcup_{m}\text{Bit}_{1,m}\quad,\quad\text{Bit}^{+}=\bigcup_{n}\text{Bit}_{n,1}\end{array}

A
planar structure
on a bit
G
is a linear order on

d_{\pm}(v)
for each
v\in G^{\circ}
, and a linear order on
d_{\pm}G
, so
that we can write

(3.10)

\begin{array}[t]{ll}&d_{-}v=\left\{0_{v}\right\}\quad,\quad d_{+}v=\left\{{\mathbf{1}}_{1}(v),\cdots,{\mathbf{1}}_{m}(v)\right\}\quad,\quad v\in C^{-}(G)\\
\\
\text{resp. }&d_{-}v=\left\{0_{1}(v),\cdots,0_{n}(v)\right\}\quad,\quad d_{+}v=\left\{1_{v}\right\}\quad\,,\quad\,v\in C^{+}(G)\end{array}

(3.11)

\begin{array}[H]{ll}d_{-}(G)=\left\{0_{1}(G),\cdots,0_{n}(G)\right\},\qquad d_{+}G=\left\{{\mathbf{1}}_{1}(G),\ldots,{\mathbf{1}}_{m}(G)\right\}.\end{array}

Thus we have the similarly defined sets of isomorphism classes preserving the
planar structure
p\text{Bit}
,
p\text{Bit}^{-}
,
p\text{Bit}^{+}
.

Remark 3.12
.

We only define the “closed” bits. One can similarly define the more
general bits by keeping two subsets
\text{out}(G)\subseteq d_{-}G
and

\text{in}(G)\subseteq d_{+}G
of the “open-stumps”.

Definition 3.13
.

For
G=\big(G^{1}\hbox to20.89pt{\vbox to28.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.33583pt\lower-9.02356pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

,

\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}^{-},\mathcal{C}^{\circ},\mathcal{C}^{+})\in\text{Coll}
, a
labeling

f:G\to\mathcal{C}
is given by functions
f^{1}:G^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
and

f^{\pm}:C^{\pm}(G)\to\mathcal{C}^{\pm}
, such that for
v\in C^{-}(G)
, resp.

C^{+}(G)
,

\begin{array}[H]{ll}&f^{-}(v)\in\mathcal{C}^{-}\left(f^{1}(0_{v});f^{1}({\mathbf{1}}_{1}(v)),\cdots,f^{1}({\mathbf{1}}_{m}(v))\right)\\
\\
\text{resp. }&f^{+}(v)\in\mathcal{C}^{+}\left(f^{1}(0_{1}(v)),\cdots,f^{1}(0_{n}(v));f^{1}({\mathbf{1}}_{v})\right)\end{array}

f^{+}(v)=f^{-}(v)\;\text{for}\;v\in C_{11}(G).

4
Formulas and
{\mathbf{1}}
-sub-Bits

Contemplating the following bits in
\text{Bit}_{2,2}
we see their structure can be
complicated:

Figure 1:
Bits in
\text{Bit}_{2,2}

We define the subset
\text{fBit}^{\pm}
of
p\text{Bit}^{\pm}
of “formulas” having

compatible
linear orders on the
d_{\pm}(v)
’s and on
d_{\pm}(G)
, by an
inductive definition obtained by grafting of bits:

(4.1)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\text{fBit}_{1,1}^{\circ}=\left\{I=\left(\overset{0}{\cdot}\leftarrow\overset{1}{\cdot}\right)\right\}\\
\\
\text{fBit}_{1,m}^{{\mathbf{1}}}=\left\{C_{m}^{-}\right\}\quad,\quad\text{fBit}_{n,1}^{1}=\left\{C_{n}^{+}\right\}\end{array}

There are two inductive steps given by compositions and actions:

For
G\in\text{fBit}_{1,m}^{k}
,
d_{+}G=\left\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{m}\right\}
,
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,m\right\}
,
n\geq 1
we have

(4.2)

G\circ_{i}C_{n}^{-}\in\text{Bit}_{1,n+m-1}^{k+1};

Picturially it is given by

G
e_{1}:=\ell_{1}
e_{m}:=\ell_{n+m-1}
C_{n}^{-}
e_{i}
\ell_{n+i-1}
\ell_{i}

Figure 2:
Inductive composition step

For
G\in\text{fBit}_{1,m}^{k}
,
m\geq n\geq 1
,
j\leq m-n+1
we obtain

(4.3)

G\begin{array}[h]{c}\scriptstyle\leftarrow\\
\scriptstyle\circ\\
\scriptstyle j\end{array}C_{n}^{+}\in\text{Bit}_{1,m-n+1}^{k+1}

pictorially it is given by

G
e_{1}:=\ell_{1}
e_{m}:=\ell_{m-n+1}
e_{j}
e_{j+n-1}
\ell_{j}

Figure 3:
Inductive action step

Elements of
\text{fBit}_{n,1}^{k}
, and
\text{fBit}^{+}
, have a similar inductive
definition.

For
G\in\text{Bit}
, a subgraph

E=\big(E^{{\mathbf{1}}}\hbox to20.89pt{\vbox to28.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.33583pt\lower-9.02356pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{13.9395pt}{8.79103pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

,
E^{i}\subseteq G^{i}
,
d_{\pm}^{E}=d_{\pm}^{G}|_{E^{{\mathbf{1}}}}
, and
such that
E
by itself is a bit will be called a
sub-bit
of
G
, and we let

\text{Bit}_{n,m}^{k}(G)
denote the sub-bits of
G
that are in

\text{Bit}_{n,m}^{k}
. In general, the collection

\text{Bit}_{n,m}(G)=\bigcup_{k}\text{Bit}_{n,m}^{k}(G)
are hard to describe, but the
sets
\text{Bit}_{1,m}^{k}(G)
and
\text{Bit}_{n,1}^{k}(G)
have a similar

inductive
definition. Beginning with

(4.4)

\begin{array}[H]{ll}\text{Bit}_{1,1}^{\circ}(G)&\equiv G^{{\mathbf{1}}}\\
\\
\text{Bit}_{1,m}^{{\mathbf{1}}}(G)&\equiv\left\{C_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}}^{-}(v);v\in C_{1,m^{\prime}}(G),\left\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}\right\}\subseteq\left\{1\cdots m^{\prime}\right\}\right\}\\
\\
\text{Bit}_{n,1}^{{\mathbf{1}}}(G)&\equiv\left\{C_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}^{+}(v);v\in C_{n^{\prime},1}(G),\left\{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\right\}\subseteq\left\{1\cdots n^{\prime}\right\}\right\}\end{array}

and using the compositions and actions graftings, for
E\in\text{Bit}_{1,m}^{k}(G)
,
(and similarly for
E\in\text{Bit}_{n,1}^{k}(G)
):

For
e_{i}\in d_{+}E
with
v=d_{+}e_{i}\in C_{1,n^{\prime}}(G)
, we have for a choice of
n\leq n^{\prime}
edges
in
d_{+}v
,

(4.5)

E\circ_{i}C_{n}^{-}(v)\in\text{Bit}_{1,m+n-1}^{k+1}(G).

For
D\subseteq d_{+}E\cap d_{-}v
,
v\in C_{n^{\prime},1}(G)
,
n^{\prime}\geq n=\#D
, we have

(4.6)

E\begin{array}[h]{l}\leftarrow\\
\,\circ\\
\scriptstyle D\end{array}C_{n}^{+}(v)\in\text{Bit}_{1,m-n+1}^{k+1}(G).

Remark 4.7
.

This is the very reason we prefer to work with “bi-operads”, and not with
the more general “properads” having operations
\mathscr{P}(b_{1}\cdots b_{n};c_{1}\cdots c_{m})
with any output
n\geq 0
and any input

m\geq 0
.

For
G\in\text{Bit}
, we denote the sub-
{\mathbf{1}}
-bits
E
of
G
together with
linear order on
d_{\pm}E
, by

\mathscr{S}_{G}^{-}=\bigcup_{k,m}\text{Bit}_{1,m}^{k}(G)\quad,\quad\mathscr{S}_{G}^{+}=\bigcup_{k,n}\text{Bit}_{n,1}^{k}(G).

5
The free Bio

p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}=\left(p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-},\mathcal{C}^{\circ},p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{+}\right)
,

(5.1)

where “isomorphisms” should preserve the planar structure and
\mathcal{C}

labeling, and also allow “degeneracies”: the replacement of an edge

\mathcal{C}^{\circ}

Similarly, the natural actions of

p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-}
and
p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{+}
on each other are given by

\mathcal{C}
-labeled bits. For example, for

\begin{array}[H]{ll}P=\left\{f:G\to\mathcal{C}\right\}\in p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\cdots b_{i-1}c_{1}\cdots c_{m}b_{i+1}\cdots b_{n})\\
\\
P^{\prime}=\left\{f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}\right\}\in p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{m};b_{i})\end{array}

we get

(5.5)

P\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,P^{\prime}:=\left\{f\cup f^{\prime}\;:\;G\coprod G^{\prime}/\approx\;\to\mathcal{C}\right\}\in p\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\cdots b_{i}\cdots b_{n})

where
\approx
means that we identify
{\mathbf{1}}_{i-1+j}(G)\approx 0_{j}(G^{\prime})
;
pictorially,

The axioms for a planar bio are easily checked. The unit of adjunction
\varepsilon:\mathcal{C}\to U\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is easy to describe:
it is the identity on objects
\mathcal{C}^{0}
, and it takes

P\in\mathcal{C}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})
), to the negative (resp. positive)
n

\mathcal{C}

by
f^{\pm}(v)=P
,

, pictorially

Given a planar bio
\mathscr{P}=\left(\mathscr{P}^{-},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\mathscr{P}^{+}\right)
,
and given an element

P=(f:G\rightarrow U\mathscr{P})\in p\mathcal{F}_{U\mathscr{P}}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n}),

we can define now
\mu^{-}(P)\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})

by induction:

(5.7)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\text{If }G\in\text{fBit}_{1,n}^{1},\quad G=C^{-}(v),\quad\mu^{-}(P)=f^{-}(v)\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n}).\\
\\
\text{If}\;G\in\text{fBit}_{1,n}^{k+1},\;\text{then either}\;G=G^{\prime}\circ_{i}C^{-}_{\ell}(v)\;\text{or}\;G=G^{\prime}\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{array}[h]{c}\;\leftarrow\\
\circ\\
\scriptstyle{j}\end{array}\textstyle C_{\ell}^{+}(v)\end{array}

with
G^{\prime}\in\text{Bit}_{1,n^{\prime}}^{k}
, and we can define

(5.8)

\mu^{-}(P)=\mu^{-}(P^{\prime})\circ_{i}f^{-}(v)\quad\text{or
respectively}\quad\mu^{-}(P)=\mu^{-}(P^{\prime})\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,_{\hskip-4.55244ptj}f^{+}(v).

It is easy to see that
\mu^{-}
is well define (independent of the order of the
operations we used in the induction steps), and similarly defining inductively

\mu^{+}:p\mathcal{F}^{+}_{U\mathscr{P}}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})
we get the co-unit map

\mu:p\mathcal{F}_{U\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

6
Bios via generators and relations

Definition 6.1
.

For
\mathscr{P}=(\mathscr{P}^{+},\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\mathscr{P}^{-})\in\text{Bio}
, an

ideal

\mathscr{I}\lhd\mathscr{P}
is a collection of equivalence
relations
\approx
on the sets
\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})
and

\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})
for all
c_{0},c_{1},\cdots,c_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
, respecting the compositions, mutual actions, and

S_{n}
-actions, so that we get a quotient bio
\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}
, with the same
objects
\left(\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}\right)^{\circ}=\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
,

(6.2)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\left(\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}\right)^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})=\mathscr{P}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})/\approx\quad,\\
\\
\left(\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}\right)^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})=\mathscr{P}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})/\approx,\end{array}

and a canonical surjective map of bios

\pi_{\mathscr{I}}:\mathscr{P}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}
identity on objects.

Given any map
\varphi\in\text{Bio}_{C}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q})
with

\varphi^{\circ}=\text{id}_{C}
we get an ideal

\ker(\varphi)\lhd\mathscr{P}
by
P\approx P^{\prime}
iff

\varphi(P)=\varphi(P^{\prime})
, and canonical factorization of
\varphi

(6.3)

\mathscr{P}
\varphi
\mathscr{Q}
\varphi(\mathscr{P})
\mathscr{P}/_{\ker(\varphi)}
\sim

Remark 6.4
.

Given ideals
\mathscr{I}_{\alpha}\lhd\mathscr{P}
, their intersection is again
an ideal
\bigcap_{\alpha}\mathscr{I}_{\alpha}\lhd\mathscr{P}
, with

P\approx P^{\prime}\mod\bigcap_{\alpha}\mathscr{I}_{\alpha}\Longleftrightarrow P\approx P^{\prime}\mod\mathscr{I}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha
.

Thus given any collection of
pairs
\left\{(P_{j},P_{j}^{\prime})\right\}
with

P_{j},P_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{\pm}(c_{0};c_{i})
we can speak of the ideal they
generate
\mathscr{I}=\left\{(P_{j},P_{j}^{\prime})\right\}_{j\in J}=\bigcap\mathscr{I}_{\alpha}
the intersection of all ideals containing all the

(P_{j},P^{\prime}_{j})
’s, and we have the canonical quotient map

\pi:\mathscr{P}\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}/\mathscr{I}:=\mathscr{P}/\left\{P_{j}\sim P_{j^{\prime}}\right\}_{j\in J}

Thus we can construct bios by “generators and relations”: the generating
objects
C^{\circ}
, and the generating (co)-operations
\left\{Q_{i}\right\}
between them gives a collection
C=(C^{-},C^{\circ},C^{+})\in\text{Coll}
, the relations
\left\{P_{j}\sim P_{j}^{\prime}\right\}

generate an ideal
\mathscr{I}\lhd\mathcal{F}_{C}
as above, and we have the
bio

(6.5)

\mathbb{F}_{C^{\circ}}\left[Q_{i}\right]/\left\{P_{j}\sim P_{j}^{\prime}\right\}:=\mathcal{F}_{C}/\mathscr{I}.

Corollary 1
.

The category
Bio
is complete and co-complete

Proof.

All limits are created in Set - for
(\mathscr{P}_{j})\in\text{Bio}^{J}
,

(6.6)

(\lim_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j})^{\circ}:=\lim_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}^{\circ}_{j}

and for
c_{0}=c_{0}^{(j)},\;c_{1}=c_{1}^{(j)}\cdots c_{n}=c_{n}^{(j)}\in\lim\limits_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j}^{\circ}
we have

(6.7)

(\lim_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j})^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n}):=\lim_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\left(\mathscr{P}_{j}^{-}(c_{0}^{(j)};c_{1}^{(j)}\cdots c_{n}^{(j)})\right)

and similarly for
(\lim_{\overset{\leftarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j})^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})
. The functor
\mathscr{P}\mapsto\mathscr{P}^{\circ}:\text{Bio}\to\text{Set}

preserves also co-limits, so let

C^{\circ}=(\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\overset{\rightarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j})^{\circ}=\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\overset{\rightarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j}^{\circ}

be the colimit in Set. The sets
\left\{\mathscr{P}_{j}^{\pm}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n}),j\in J,c_{0},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\in\mathscr{P}_{j}^{\circ}\right\}
induce a collection

C=(C^{-},C^{\circ},C^{+})
. The relations in each
\mathscr{P}_{j},j\in J
, give
relations on these generators. Also for arrows
\varphi^{\prime}:j\to j^{\prime}
,
\varphi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}:j\to j^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
in
J
with the same domain, and
for any (co).operation
P\in\mathscr{P}_{j}^{\pm}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})
we have the
relation
\varphi_{\ast}^{{}^{\prime}}(P)\sim\varphi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{\ast}{(P)}
; these give all the
relations and

(6.8)

\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\overset{\rightarrow}{J}}\mathscr{P}_{j}=\mathcal{F}_{C}/_{\left\{\varphi^{{}^{\prime}}_{\ast}(P)\sim\varphi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{\ast}(P)\right\}}

∎

7
Bipo
\equiv
Bi-Pre-Orders

Definition 7.1
.

For a set
C
let
M(C)=\coprod_{n\geq 0}C^{n}
denote the free (associative,
unital) monoid on
C
; its elements are “words”

\overline{c}=c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\in C^{n}
of “length”
n\geq 0
,
including the unit: the empty word
\phi
(
n=0
).

A
planar Bipo

(= Bi-pre-order) is a set
C
together with two relations
R^{-}\subseteq C\times M(C)
and
R^{+}\subseteq M(C)\times C
; we write:

c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Leftrightarrow(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n})\in R^{-}\quad,\quad c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq^{\prime}c_{0}\Leftrightarrow(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0})\in R^{+},

such that

(7.2)

c\leq c\quad\text{and}\quad c\leq^{\prime}c\quad\text{for all}\quad c\in C.

For
n=1
we have

(7.3)

c_{0}\leq c_{1}\Longleftrightarrow c_{0}\leq^{\prime}c_{1},

and we write
\leq
for
\leq^{\prime}
with no danger of confusion.

(7.4)

\begin{array}[t]{l}b_{0}\leq b_{1}\cdots b_{n},\quad b_{i}\leq\overline{c}_{i}=c_{i1}\cdots c_{im_{i}}\Longrightarrow b_{0}\leq\overline{c}_{1}\cdots\overline{c}_{n}\\
\\
b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\leq b_{0},\quad\overline{c}_{i}=c_{i1}\cdots c_{im_{i}}\leq b_{i}\Longrightarrow\overline{c}_{1}\cdots\overline{c}_{n}\leq b_{0}\end{array}

(7.5)

\begin{array}[t]{l}b_{0}\leq\overline{c}_{1}\cdots\overline{c}_{n},\quad\overline{c}_{i}=c_{i1}\cdots c_{im_{i}}\leq b_{i}\Longrightarrow b_{0}\leq b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\\
\\
\overline{c}_{1}\cdots\overline{c}_{n}\leq b_{0},\quad b_{i}\leq\overline{c}_{i}=c_{i1}\cdots c_{im_{i}}\Longrightarrow b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\leq b_{0}\end{array}

(Notice that (
7.3
) follows from (
7.2
), (
7.4
) and
(
7.5
)).

A map of planar bipo
\varphi:C\to C^{\prime}
is a map of sets such that

(7.6)

\begin{array}[H]{ll}c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Longrightarrow\varphi(c_{0})\leq\varphi(c_{1})\cdots\varphi(c_{n})\\
\\
c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\Longrightarrow\varphi(c_{1})\cdots\varphi(c_{n})\leq\varphi(c_{0})\end{array}

Thus we have a category
pBipo
.

A planar bipo
C\in p\text{Bipo}
is
called
symmetric
if for
c_{0},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\in C
,

(7.7)

\begin{array}[H]{l}c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Longleftrightarrow c_{0}\leq c_{\sigma(1)}\cdots c_{\sigma(n)}\quad\text{any $\sigma\in S_{n}$}\\
\\
c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\Longleftrightarrow c_{\sigma(1)}\cdots c_{\sigma(n)}\leq c_{0}\quad\text{any $\sigma\in S_{n}$}\end{array}

i.e. the relations
\leq
,
\leq^{\prime}
are induced from similar relations on the free

commutative
monoid on
C
,

(7.8)

\text{CM}(C)=\coprod\limits_{n\geq 0}C^{n}/_{S_{n}}

via the cannonical map
\pi:M(C)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\text{CM}(C)
.

We let
\text{Bipo}\subseteq p\text{Bipo}
denote the full subcategory of symmetric - bipos, and we call its
objects simply “bipos”.

We say a bipo
C\in\text{Bipo}
is
fermionic

if for
c_{0},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\in C
,

(7.9)

\begin{array}[H]{l}c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Longrightarrow c_{i}\not=c_{j}\;\text{for
$i\not=j$, $i,j>0$.}\\
\\
c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\Longrightarrow c_{i}\not=c_{j}\;\text{for
$i\not=j$, $i,j>0$.}\end{array}

We say
C
is
simple
if,

(7.10)

c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\;\;\text{and}\;\;c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\Rightarrow c_{0}=c_{1}=\cdots=c_{n}.

We say
C
is
closed
if,

(7.11)

c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n},\;\;\;n>1\Rightarrow c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n-1}

and similarly if

c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\quad,\quad n>1\Rightarrow c_{1}\cdots c_{n-1}\leq c_{0}.

Given
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{n}\in\text{Bipo}(C,D)\equiv D^{C}
define

(7.12)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\varphi_{0}\leq\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\Longleftrightarrow\varphi_{0}(c)\leq_{D}\varphi_{1}(c)\cdots\varphi_{n}(c)\quad\text{for all $c\in C$}\\
\\
\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\leq\varphi_{0}\Longleftrightarrow\varphi_{1}(c)\cdots\varphi_{n}(c)\leq_{D}\varphi_{0}(c)\quad\text{for all $c\in C$}\end{array}

This makes
D^{C}
into a bipo, the “internal Hom”.

Given bipos
\leq_{i}
on the same underlying set
C
, the intersection

\bigcap\limits_{i}\leq_{i}
is again a bipo,
c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Leftrightarrow c_{0}\leq_{i}c_{1}\cdots c_{n}
for all
i
. Thus we can talk about the bipo generated by
certain relations

\left\{c_{0}^{(i)}\leq c_{1}^{(i)}\cdots c_{n_{i}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i\in J^{-}}\cup\left\{c_{1}^{\prime(i)}\cdots c_{n_{i}}^{\prime(i)}\leq c_{0}^{\prime(i)}\right\}_{i\in J^{+}}

We let
B\otimes C
denote the bipo on
B\times C
, for
B,C\in\text{Bipo}
,
generated by the following relations

(7.13)

\begin{array}[H]{l}(b,c_{0})\leq(b,c_{1})\cdots(b,c_{n})\quad\text{all $b\in B$,
$c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}$ in C}\\
\\
(b_{0},c)\leq(b_{1},c)\cdots(b_{n},c)\quad\text{all $c\in C$,
$b_{0}\leq b_{1}\cdots b_{n}$ in $B$}\\
\\
(b,c_{1})\cdots(b,c_{n})\leq(b,c_{0})\quad\text{all $b\in B$,
$c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}$ in $C$}\\
\\
(b_{1},c)\cdots(b_{n},c)\leq(b_{0},c)\quad\text{all $c\in C$,
$b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\leq b_{0}$ in $B$}.\end{array}

Proposition 1
.

We have the adjunction

\text{Bipo}(B,D^{C})\equiv\text{Bipo}(B\otimes C,D)

via the usual Set adjunction
\varphi_{b}(c)\leftrightarrow\varphi(b,c)
, and thus
Bipo
is complete, co-complete, closed
symmetric monoidal category.

Proof.

Limits in Bipo are created in Set. For co-limits

\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.3612pt}}C_{j}
, we take the co-limit in Set
of the underlying sets
C_{j}
, and than the bipo generated by all the
relations in the
C_{j}
’s. The functor
B,C\mapsto B\otimes C
is clearly
symmetric monoidal, and it is closed because of the adjunction.
∎

For a bipo
B
, we have the bio
\ell_{!}B
, with

\ell_{!}B^{\circ}:=B
, and

(7.14)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\ell_{!}B^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\cdots b_{n})=\left\{\begin{array}[H]{cl}\left\{\ast\right\}&b_{0}\leq b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\\
\\
\phi&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\\
\ell_{!}B^{+}(b_{1}\cdots b_{n};b_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[H]{cl}\left\{\ast\right\}&b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\leq b_{0}\\
\\
\phi&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

This gives a full and faithful embedding
\ell_{!}:\text{Bipo}\subseteq\text{Bio}
, where the image are the bios having at most one point in every
hom - set (just like the pre-orders are the categories having at most one
point in every hom-set). The functor
\ell_{!}
has a left adjoint

\ell^{\ast}:\text{Bio}\to\text{Bipo}
, for
\mathscr{P}\in\text{Bio}
,

\ell^{\ast}\mathscr{P}
is the bipo with the same objects as
\mathscr{P}
and for

b_{0},\cdots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
,

(7.15)

\begin{array}[H]{l}b_{0}\leq_{\ell^{\ast}\mathscr{P}}b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\Longleftrightarrow\mathscr{P}^{-}(b_{0};b_{1}\cdots b_{n})\not=\phi\\
\\
b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\leq_{\ell^{\ast}\mathscr{P}}b_{0}\Longleftrightarrow\mathscr{P}^{+}(b_{1}\cdots b_{n};b_{0})\not=\phi.\end{array}

8
Natural transformations

Definition 8.1
.

Given
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\in\text{Bio}(\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})
a

natural transformation

\alpha^{-}\in\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}(\varphi_{0};\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
is a map
\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}\ni q\mapsto\alpha^{-}_{q}\in\mathscr{R}^{-}(\varphi_{0}(q);\varphi_{1}(q)\cdots\varphi_{n}(q))

such that for
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{-}(q_{0};q_{1}\cdots q_{m})
we have
interchange
:

(8.2)

\varphi_{0}^{-}(Q)\circ\left(\alpha^{-}_{q_{j}}\right)\circ\sigma_{n,m}=\alpha_{q_{0}}^{-}\circ\left(\varphi_{i}^{-}(Q)\right)

with
\sigma_{n,m}\in S_{n\cdot m}
the “rows-columns-interchange”

(8.3)

\sigma_{n,m}(a\cdot n+b+1)=b\cdot m+a+1,\quad 0\leq a<m,\quad 0\leq b<n

Similarly
\alpha^{+}\in\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n};\varphi_{0})
is a map

\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}\ni q\mapsto\alpha_{q}^{+}\in\mathscr{R}^{+}(\varphi_{1}(q)\cdots\varphi_{n}(q);\varphi_{0}(q))
such that for
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{+}(q_{1}\cdots q_{m};q_{0})
we have
interchange:

(8.4)

\sigma_{n,m}\circ(\alpha_{q_{j}}^{+})\circ\varphi^{+}_{0}(Q)=\left(\varphi_{i}^{+}(Q)\right)\circ\alpha_{q_{0}}^{+}.

We have composition and mutual actions of natural transformations given pointwise

(8.5)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\left(\alpha^{-}\circ(\alpha_{i}^{-})\right)_{q}=\alpha^{-}_{q}\circ\left((\alpha_{i}^{-})_{q}\right)\quad,\quad\left((\alpha_{i}^{+})\circ\alpha^{+}\right)_{q}=\left((\alpha_{i}^{+})_{q}\right)\circ\alpha^{+}_{q}\\
\\
\left(\alpha^{-}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,(\alpha_{i}^{+})\right)_{q}=\alpha^{-}_{q}\,\overleftarrow{\circ}\,\left((\alpha_{i}^{+})_{q}\right)\quad,\quad\left((\alpha_{i}^{-})\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\alpha^{+}\right)_{q}=\left((\alpha_{i}^{-})_{q}\right)\,\overrightarrow{\circ}\,\alpha^{+}_{q}.\end{array}

These make
\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}
into a bio with objects
\left(\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}\right)^{\circ}=\text{Bio}(\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})
, and gives the internal hom functor

(8.6)

\left(\text{Bio}\right)^{\text{op}}\times\text{Bio}\to\text{Bio}\quad,\quad(\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})\mapsto\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}.

9
Billinear maps and tensor products

Fix
\varphi\in\text{Bio}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}})
. On objects
\varphi^{\circ}
gives for each

p\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
an object
\varphi^{\circ}(p)
of
\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}
, i.e.

\varphi^{\circ}(p)\in\text{Bio}(\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})
, which on objects gives for
q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}
the
object
\varphi^{\circ}(p,q)\in\mathscr{R}^{\circ}
, thus we have a function

(9.1)

\varphi^{\circ}:\mathscr{P}^{\circ}\times\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{R}^{\circ}.

For
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\pm}
,
\varphi^{\circ}(p)
associate
\varphi^{\pm}(p,Q)\in\mathscr{R}^{\pm}
,
preserving composition and actions. For
P\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(p_{0};p_{1}\cdots p_{n})
, (resp.

P\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(p_{1}\cdots p_{n};p_{0})
),
\varphi
associates the natural transformation

(9.2)

\begin{array}[H]{c}\varphi^{-}(P)\in\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}\big(\varphi^{\circ}(p_{0});\varphi^{\circ}(p_{1})\cdots\varphi^{\circ}(p_{n})\big),\\
\\
\Big(\text{resp. }\varphi^{+}(P)\in\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}}\big(\varphi^{\circ}(p_{1})\cdots\varphi^{\circ}(p_{n});\varphi^{\circ}(p_{0})\big)\,\Big),\end{array}

thus for
q\in Q^{\circ}
we have
\varphi^{-}(P,q)\in\mathscr{R}^{-}\big(\varphi^{\circ}(p_{0},q);\varphi^{\circ}(p_{1},q),\cdots,\varphi^{\circ}(p_{n},q)\big)
,
\Big(
resp.
\varphi^{+}(P,q)\in\mathscr{R}^{+}\big(\varphi^{\circ}(p_{1},q)\cdots\varphi^{\circ}(p_{n},q),\varphi^{\circ}(p_{0},q)\big)\Big)
and
since the composition and actions are defined poinwise, for fixed
q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}
,

P\mapsto\varphi(P,q)
preserves compositions and actions. Moreover, we have interchange:
for
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{-}(q_{0};q_{1}\cdots q_{m})
,
\Big(
resp.
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{+}(q_{1}\cdots q_{m};q_{0})\Big)
,

(9.3)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\varphi^{-}(p_{0},Q)\circ\big(\varphi^{-}(P,q_{j})\big)=\varphi^{-}(P,q_{0})\circ\big(\varphi^{-}(p_{i},Q)\big)\circ\sigma_{n,m},\\
\end{array}

\Big(
resp.

\sigma_{n,m}\circ\big(\varphi^{+}(P,q_{j})\big)\circ\varphi^{+}(p_{0},Q)=\big(\varphi^{+}(p_{i},Q)\big)\circ\varphi^{+}(P,q_{0})\Big)
.

Writing
\varphi_{p}(-):=\varphi(p,-)
,
\psi_{q}(-):=\varphi(-,q)
, we have

(9.4)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\text{Bio}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}})\equiv\text{Bill}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q};\mathscr{R}):\overset{\text{def}}{=}\\
\\
\left\{\begin{array}[H]{l}(\varphi,\psi)\in\text{Bio}(\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})^{\mathscr{P}^{\circ}}\times\text{Bio}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{R})^{\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}}\quad,\quad\varphi_{p}(q)=\psi_{q}(p)\;\text{for}\;p\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ},\;q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}\\
\\
\varphi_{p_{0}}^{-}(Q)\circ\big(\psi_{q_{j}}^{-}(P)\big)=\psi_{q_{0}}^{-}(P)\circ\big(\varphi_{p_{i}}^{-}(Q)\big)\circ\sigma_{m,n}\quad,\quad\begin{array}[t]{l}P\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(p_{0};p_{1}\cdots p_{n})\;,\\
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{-}(q_{0};q_{1}\cdots q_{m})\end{array}\\
\\
\sigma_{n,m}\circ\big(\psi_{q_{j}}^{+}(P)\big)\circ\varphi_{p_{0}}^{+}(Q)=\big(\varphi_{p_{i}}^{+}(Q)\big)\circ\psi_{q_{0}}^{+}(P)\quad,\quad\begin{array}[t]{l}P\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(p_{1}\cdots p_{n};p_{0}),\\
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{+}(q_{1}\cdots q_{m};q_{0})\end{array}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

For fixed
\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q}
the functor
\text{Bio}\to\text{Set}
,

\mathscr{R}\mapsto\text{Bill}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q};\mathscr{R})
is representable by a bio
\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}
,
and we get the adjunction

(9.5)

\text{Bio}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{R}^{\mathscr{Q}})\equiv\text{Bill}(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q};\mathscr{R})\equiv\text{Bio}(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{R})

Here
\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}
is defined by generators and relations:

(9.6)

\text{on objects}\quad\left(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}\right)^{\circ}:=\mathscr{P}^{\circ}\times\mathscr{Q}^{\circ};

For
q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}
we have generators
\left\{P\otimes q,P\in\mathscr{P}^{\pm}\right\}
, and for
p\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
we have generators
\left\{p\otimes Q,Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\pm}\right\}
; The generators
\left\{P\otimes q\right\}_{P\in\mathscr{P}^{\pm}}
,
(resp
\left\{p\otimes Q\right\}_{Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\pm}}
) satisfying the relations of

\mathscr{P}
(resp. of
\mathscr{Q}
), so that for each
q\in\mathscr{Q}^{\circ}
(resp.

p\in\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
)
j_{q}:\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}
,
j_{q}(P)=P\otimes q

(resp.
j_{p}:\mathscr{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}
,
j_{p}(Q)=p\otimes Q
)
is a map of bios, and we have the interchange relations:

(9.7)

\begin{array}[H]{l}(p_{0}\otimes Q)\circ(P\otimes q_{j})=(P\otimes q_{0})\circ(p_{i}\otimes Q)\circ\sigma_{n,m}\quad,\quad\begin{array}[t]{l}P\in\mathscr{P}^{-}(p_{0};p_{1}\cdots p_{n})\;,\\
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{-}(q_{0},q_{1}\cdots q_{m})\end{array}\\
\\
\sigma_{n,m}\circ(P\otimes q_{j})\circ(p_{0}\otimes Q)=(p_{i}\otimes Q)\circ(P\otimes q_{0})\quad,\quad\begin{array}[t]{l}P\in\mathscr{P}^{+}(p_{1}\cdots p_{n};p_{0})\;,\\
Q\in\mathscr{Q}^{+}(q_{1}\cdots q_{m};q_{0})\end{array}\end{array}

The functor
\text{Bio}\times\text{Bio}\to\text{Bio}
,
(\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q})\mapsto\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q}
, makes Bio into a symmetric monoidal category with unit

(9.8)

I:I^{\circ}=\left\{e\right\},\;\;I(e,e)=\left\{\text{id}_{e}\right\},\;\;I^{-}(e;\underbrace{e\cdots e}_{n})=\phi=I^{+}(\underbrace{e\cdots e}_{n};e)\;\;\text{for $n>1$; }

so
\text{Bio}(I,\mathscr{P})\equiv\mathscr{P}^{\circ}
, and
\text{Bio}/I\equiv\text{Cat}
. Thus we have,

Theorem 1
.

Bio is a closed symmetric monoidal category.

Remark 9.9
.

We have the operads
(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q})^{\pm}
, and there are map of operads

\mathscr{P}^{-}\otimes_{BW}\mathscr{Q}^{-}\longrightarrow(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q})^{-}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{P}^{+}\otimes_{BW}\mathscr{Q}^{+}\longrightarrow(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{Q})^{+}

with
\otimes_{BV}
the Boardman-Vogt tensor product; but these maps are
not in general isomorphism!

10
From Bits to Bios

Fix a bit

. Recall the

{\mathbf{1}}
-sub bits
B
of
G
, with linear order on
d_{\pm}B
,

\mathscr{S}_{G}^{\pm}
, and associate with
G
the bio
\mathscr{S}_{G}
:

(10.1)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\mathscr{S}_{G}^{\circ}:=G^{{\mathbf{1}}}\quad,\quad\text{for $c_{0},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\in G^{{\mathbf{1}}}$}\\
\\
\mathscr{S}_{G}^{-}(c_{0};c_{1}\cdots c_{n}):=\left\{B\in\mathscr{S}_{G}^{-},d_{-}B=\{c_{0}\},d_{+}B=\left\{c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\right\}\right\}\\
\\
\mathscr{S}_{G}^{+}(c_{1}\cdots c_{n};c_{0}):=\left\{B\in\mathscr{S}_{G}^{+},d_{-}B=\left\{c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\right\},d_{+}B=\left\{c_{0}\right\}\right\}\end{array}

The compositions and actions are given by grafting sub-bits. The
S_{n}
-actions are
given by reordering
d_{\pm}B
, and is free. Note that the associated bipo

\ell^{*}\mathscr{S}_{G}
, given by the set
G^{{\mathbf{1}}}
and

(10.2)

\begin{array}[H]{l}c_{0}\leq c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\Longleftrightarrow\exists B\in\mathscr{S}_{G}^{-}\quad,\quad d_{-}B=\left\{c_{0}\right\}\quad,\quad d_{+}B=\left\{c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\right\}\\
\\
c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\leq c_{0}\Longleftrightarrow\exists B\in\mathscr{S}_{G}^{+}\quad,\quad d_{-}B=\left\{c_{1}\cdots c_{n}\right\}\quad,\quad d_{+}B=\left\{c_{0}\right\}\end{array}

is simple, closed, and fermionic.

A map
of bios
f:\mathscr{S}_{G}\to\mathscr{S}_{H}
,
G,H\in\text{Bit}
, is given by

(10.3)

\begin{array}[H]{l}f^{{\mathbf{1}}}:G^{{\mathbf{1}}}\to H^{{\mathbf{1}}}\;\text{and}\;f^{\circ}:G^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}^{\pm}_{G}\;\text{with}\;d_{\pm}f^{\circ}(v)=f^{{\mathbf{1}}}(d_{\pm}v)\;\text{for}\;v\in G^{\circ}.\end{array}

(10.9)

:

f

\pi
\tilde{f}

where
\pi
is flat by definition, and is surjective on edges, and
\tilde{f}
is
injective on edges (hence also flat).

Note that when
n=0
(resp.
m=0
) we are pruning
G
near
d_{+}G
(resp.
d_{-}G
). Note
that
G^{e}
have one less
edge
than
G
(and can have more vertices!).

Note that if
f\in\text{Bit}(G,H)
with
f^{1}
injective, and
f
is also a map of graphs
(so that
f^{0}(v)\subseteq C\left(\tilde{f}^{0}(v)\right)
,
\tilde{f}^{0}:G^{0}\to H^{0}
), then
f
is the composition of the
0
-faces

\partial_{e},e\in H^{1}\setminus G^{1}
.

Finally, we have the
inner-faces
, where we contract edges,
and they come again in
4
types:

\begin{array}[H]{c}\left(\begin{array}[H]{l}-\quad,\quad-\\
m\quad,\quad n\end{array}\right)\;:\\
m,n\geq 1\end{array}
e
m
n
\partial_{e}^{\,-}
m+n-1

– composition face

\begin{array}[H]{c}\left(\begin{array}[H]{l}+\quad,\quad+\\
m\quad,\quad n\end{array}\right)\;:\\
m,n\geq 1\end{array}
e
m
n
\partial_{e}^{\,+}
m+n-1

Figure 9:
+ composition face

\begin{array}[H]{c}\left(\begin{array}[H]{l}-\quad,\quad+\\
m\quad,\quad n\end{array}\right)\;:\\
m\geq n\geq 1\end{array}
e_{i}
.
.
.
e_{1}
e_{n}
m
\partial_{e}^{\,\leftarrow}
m-n+1

Figure 10:
- action face

\begin{array}[H]{c}\left(\begin{array}[H]{l}-\quad,\quad+\\
m\quad,\quad n\end{array}\right)\;:\\
1\leq m\leq n\end{array}
e_{i}
.
.
.
e_{1}
e_{m}
n
\partial_{e}^{\,\rightarrow}
n-m+1

Figure 11:
+ action face

Remark 11.6
.

We will only need the full subcategory

\text{Bit}_{\text{con}}\subseteq\text{Bit}
consisting of

G\in\text{Bit}
with
G
a
connected
graph (Indeed,

1
-bits are always connected!). The maps in
\text{Bit}_{\text{con}}

have similar description via faces and degeneracies, but one has to be
careful when taking a
0
-face not to disconnect the graph.

12
The categroy of Bital sets
b
Set

We let
\text{bSet}:=\text{Set}^{\text{Bit}^{\text{op}}}
denote the category of

Bital-sets
. Its objects are
X=\left\{X_{G}\right\}_{G\in\text{Bit}}
,  a
collection of sets one for each
G\in\text{Bit}
, and function

\varphi^{\ast}:X_{H}\to X_{G}
for each
\varphi\in\text{Bit}(G,H)
,
\left(\psi\circ\varphi\right)^{\ast}=\varphi^{\ast}\circ\psi^{\ast}
,

\text{id}_{G}^{\ast}=\text{id}_{X_{G}}
;

maps   are   natural   transformations
f:X\to Y
,

f_{G}\in\text{Set}(X_{G},Y_{G})
,
f_{G}\circ\varphi^{\ast}=\varphi^{\ast}\circ f_{H}
for
\varphi\in\text{Bit}(G,H)
.

We call an element
x\in X_{G}\;\text{a "\lx@text@underline{bitex} of type $G$" (plural: "bitices")}

The category
b
Set is complete and cocomplete, and for

F=\left\{F_{d}\right\}\in\text{bSet}^{D}

the (co-)limit is obtained pointwise

\left(\mathop{\text{(co)lim}}\limits_{d\in D}F_{d}\right)_{G}=\mathop{\text{(co)lim}}\limits_{d\in D}\left(F_{d}\right)_{G}
.

Indeed for
G\in\text{Bit}
, the functor
\delta_{G}^{\ast}:\text{bSet}\to\text{Set}
,

\delta_{G}^{\ast}(X)=X_{G}
, has both a left and a right adjoints.

Every
X\in\text{bSet}
is a canonical colimit of representable functors

(12.1)

X=\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\begin{array}[b]{c}\scriptstyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 14.72241pt}\\
\scriptstyle(\mathscr{Y}_{G}\to X)\end{array}\in\text{Bit}/X}\mathscr{Y}_{G}

\mathscr{Y}:\text{Bit}\,\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

.

Figure 12
:
Realization functors

The category
b
Set has an involution
X\mapsto X^{\text{op}}
with

(X^{\text{op}})_{G}:=X_{G^{\text{op}}}
, compatible with the involution

\mathscr{P}\mapsto\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}}
on
\text{Bio}:N(\mathscr{P}^{\text{op}})=N(\mathscr{P})^{\text{op}}
,

\tau(X^{\text{op}})=\tau(X)^{\text{op}}
.

13
The symmetric “monoidal” structure on
b
Set

The closed symmetric monoidal structure on Bio induces a closed “almost-monoidal”
symmetric structure on
b
Set via Day convolution

(13.1)

X\otimes Y:=\hskip-31.29802pt\mathop{\text{colim}}\limits_{\hskip 28.45274pt\begin{array}[t]{l}\scriptstyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 14.72241pt}\\
\scriptstyle{(\mathscr{Y}_{G}\to X)}\in\text{Bit}/X\\
\scriptstyle(\mathscr{Y}_{H}\to Y)\in\text{Bit}/Y\end{array}}\hskip-25.60747ptN\left(\mathscr{S}_{G}\otimes\mathscr{S}_{H}\right)

with inner hom

(13.2)

\left(Y^{X}\right)_{G}:=\text{bSet}\left(X\otimes\mathscr{Y}_{G},Y\right)

and adjunction

(13.3)

\text{bSet}(X\otimes Y,Z)\equiv\text{bSet}(X,Z^{Y})

so that
\_\otimes\_
commutes with co-limits. This symmetric structure
X\otimes Y\cong Y\otimes X
, is Not associative: we have inclusions that need not be
equalities:

(13.4)

(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\subseteq X\otimes Y\otimes Z\supseteq X\otimes(Y\otimes Z).

Remark 13.5
.

The tensor product of representable can be described as in
[
17
]
via
“perculations”. We have

(13.6)

\mathscr{Y}_{G}\otimes\mathscr{Y}_{H}\equiv N(\mathscr{S}_{G}\otimes\mathscr{S}_{H})=\bigcup\limits_{K}\mathscr{Y}_{K}

the union taken over all “shuffles“
K
of
G
and
H
, with
K^{1}\subseteq G^{1}\times H^{1}
,
K^{0}\subseteq G^{1}\times H^{0}\coprod G^{0}\times H^{1}
. For

G\in\text{Bit}_{m_{G},n_{G}}
,
H\in\text{Bit}_{m_{H},n_{H}}
, the shuffles
come with a partial order, with the biggest shuffle
K_{\max}
obtained by putting

m_{H}
copies of
G
on the left of
n_{G}
copies of
H
, and grafting the

m_{H}\times n_{G}
edges together:
m_{H}\cdot d_{+}G\sim n_{G}\cdot d_{-}{H}

(13.7)

K_{\max}=G\times d_{-}H\coprod\limits_{d_{+}G\times d_{-}H}d_{+}G\times H

Similarly, the smallest shuffle is

(13.8)

K_{\min}=d_{-}G\times H\coprod\limits_{d_{-}G\times d_{+}H}G\times d_{+}H

We go from
K_{\max}
down to
K_{\min}
in this partial order via the
”perculation moves“, moving
G
(co)-operations
G^{0}\times H^{1}
to the right
of
H
(co)-operations
G^{1}\times H^{0}
, replacing a copy of
A\times d_{-}B\hskip-11.38109pt\coprod\limits_{d_{+}A\times d_{-}B}\hskip-11.38109ptd_{+}A\times B
by a copy of

d_{-}A\times B\hskip-11.38109pt\coprod\limits_{d_{-}A\times d_{+}B}\hskip-11.38109ptA\times d_{+}B
for sub-bits
A\in\text{Bit}_{m^{\prime},n}(G)
,
B\in\text{Bit}_{m,n^{\prime}}(H)
.

Graphically, with
A_{j}\cong A
,
B_{i}\cong B
,

m\cdot m^{\prime}\left\{\begin{array}[H]{lll}\hbox to158.01pt{\vbox to125.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.50142pt\lower-10.49872pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

14
The image of
N:\text{Bio}\,\hookrightarrow\,\text{bSet}

For a face map in Bit,
\partial_{e}:G^{e}\hookrightarrow G
, we let

(14.1)

\partial_{e}\mathscr{Y}_{G}=\text{Image}\left\{\partial_{e*}:\mathscr{Y}_{G^{e}}\to\mathscr{Y}_{G}\right\}\subseteq\mathscr{Y}_{G}

so

\left(\partial_{e}\mathscr{Y}_{G}\right)_{K}=\left\{\partial_{e}\circ\varphi,\varphi\in\text{Bit}\left(K,G^{e}\right)\right\}

We write the boundary,

(14.2)

\partial\mathscr{Y}_{G}:=\bigcup_{\partial_{e}\in\Phi(G)}\partial_{e}\mathscr{Y}_{G}\subseteq\mathscr{Y}_{G}

and the
\partial_{e}
-horn

(14.3)

\Lambda^{\partial_{e}}\mathscr{Y}_{G}:=\bigcup_{\partial^{\prime}\in\Phi(G)\setminus\left\{\partial_{e}\right\}}\partial^{\prime}\mathscr{Y}_{G}\subseteq\partial\mathscr{Y}_{G}.

and spine

(14.4)

\text{Sp}\,\mathscr{Y}_{G}:=\bigcup_{v}\mathscr{Y}_{C(v)},

the union over all
v\in C^{\pm}(G)
,
C(v)
the corolla at
v
.

Definition 14.5
.

X\in\text{bSet}
is a (strict) inner Kan bital set if the diagram has a
(unique) filling

(14.6)

f:\Lambda^{\partial}\mathscr{Y}_{G}
X
\mathscr{Y}_{G}

for every (
\pm
-composition or
\overset{\leftrightarrows}{o}
-action) inner face

\partial
. The inner Kan bital sets will be also called
\infty
-bios and we
denote them by
\infty\text{Bio}\subseteq\text{bSet}
.

For
\mathscr{P}\in\text{Bio}
,
X=N(\mathscr{P})
,
X_{G}=\text{Bio}(\mathscr{S}_{G},\mathscr{P})
, a bitex
x\in X_{G}
is given by compatible

(14.7)

\begin{array}[H]{l}f_{x}^{1}:G^{1}\longrightarrow\mathscr{P}^{0}\\
\\
f_{x}^{\pm}:\mathscr{S}_{G}^{\pm}\longrightarrow\mathscr{P}^{\pm}\quad,\quad f_{x}^{1}(d_{\pm}B)=d_{\pm}f(B),\end{array}

and
f_{x}^{\pm}
is completely determined (by compositions and actions) by its
restrictions
f_{x}^{\pm}:C^{\pm}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{P}^{\pm}
to the corollas of

G
,
C^{\pm}(G)
being the generators of
\mathscr{S}_{G}
.

A map
f:\Lambda^{e}\mathscr{Y}_{G}\to X=N(\mathscr{P})
again gives such compatible data
f^{1}:G^{1}\to\mathscr{P}^{0}
,
f^{\pm}:C^{\pm}(G)\to\mathscr{P}^{\pm}
, and is determined uniquely by
this data, so

(14.8)

\begin{array}[H]{l}\text{bSet}\left(\text{Sp}\mathscr{Y}_{G},X\right)=\text{bSet}\left(\Lambda^{e}\mathscr{Y}_{G},X\right)\equiv\text{bSet}\left(\mathscr{Y}_{G},X\right)\equiv X_{G}\end{array}

for
X
(the nerve of) a bio, and
X
is strict inner Kan.
More precisely we have

Theorem 2
.

For
X\in\text{bSet}
, the following are equivalent

(1)

X
is strict inner Kan

(2)

X\cong N(\mathscr{P})
for
\mathscr{P}\in\text{Bio}

(3)

The unit of adjunction
X\xrightarrow{\sim}{}N\tau(X)
is an
isomorphism

(4)

For every
G\in\text{Bit}
, the restriction

X_{G}=\text{bSet}\left(\mathscr{Y}_{G},X\right)\longrightarrow\text{bSet}\left(\text{Sp}\mathscr{Y}_{G},X\right)

is a bijection.

And so
N
gives a full and faithful embedding of

Bio
into
bSet
with image the strict Kan
bital sets.

15
The homotopy functor
\tau=\text{ho}:\infty\cdot\text{Bio}\rightarrow\text{Bio}

For
X\in\infty\text{Bio}
the associated Bio
\tau(X)
has a homotopical description as

\text{ho}(X)
which we next briefly describe. The objects of
\text{ho}(X)
are

(15.1)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\overset{1}{\bullet}\right).

Thus for a bitex
x\in X_{G}
, and an edge
e\in G^{1}
, the face
\delta_{e}:I\rightarrow G
gives
x|_{e}:=\delta_{e}^{*}(x)\in\text{ho}(X)^{0}
.

and
h
restrict to
x,y,z
under the appropriate face maps.

For
x,z\in X_{C_{m}^{-}}
,
x\circ_{i}1_{e_{i}}\approx z
is independent of
i
, and gives an
equivalence relation on
X_{C_{m}^{-}}
.

We define for
x_{i}\in\text{ho}(X)^{0}

(15.3)

\text{ho}(X)^{-}(x_{0};x_{1}\cdots x_{n}):=\left\{x\in X_{C_{n}^{-}},x|_{e_{i}}=x_{i}\right\}/\approx

and similarly
\text{ho}(X)^{+}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n};x_{0})
.
For
x\in\text{ho}(X)^{-}(x_{0};x_{1}\cdots x_{n})
,
y\in\text{ho}(X)^{-}(x_{i};y_{1},\cdots y_{m})
we can
define
x\circ_{i}y:=z
if
h:x\circ_{i}y\approx z
; the existence of
h
, and
z
,
follows from filling the
\ell_{0}=e_{i}
horn in (
15.2
). This is well defined, independent of
the representatives chosen, and makes
\text{ho}(X)^{-}
into an operad, and similarly for

\text{ho}(X)^{+}
. For
m\geq n+j-1

we say
x\overleftarrow{\circ}_{\hskip-2.84526ptj}\,y\approx z
, if there is a homotopy

h:x\overleftarrow{\circ}_{\hskip-2.84526ptj}\,y\approx z
, i.e. if there is an element

h
,

that restrict to
x,y,z
under the obvious face maps. Again, given
x,y
the existence of

h
and
z
, follows from filling the
\ell_{k}=e_{j+k-1}
horn in (

), and putting

is well defined, independent of the

x,y,z

\text{ho}(X)^{+}
on

\text{ho}(X)^{-}
. Similarly we have the action of
\text{ho}(X)^{-}
on

, and it is easy to see these are linear so that we have a bio

\text{ho}(X)
